Assessment of sustained efficacy and safety of a 300IR 5-grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy tablet (SLIT) in adults with grass pollen induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis during a 3-year treatment study; Background: In single-season clinical trials, once-daily pre-coseasonal treatment with a 300IR 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet was shown to be safe and effective in adults and children. The aim of this study was to evaluate the sustained efficacy and safety of pre-coseasonal treatment with 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet in adults with grass pollen allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Methods: 633 patients aged between 18 and 51 years were included in a DBPC, international, multi-centre Phase III trial to evaluate the sustained efficacy and safety of the 300IR 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet over 3 consecutive seasons. Patients were randomized to a placebo and 2 active groups, receiving either 4 or 2 months of pre-seasonal treatment and co-seasonal treatment. Use of rescue medication was permitted. The primary efficacy endpoint was the Average Adjusted Symptom Score (AAdSS, a score that integrates the symptoms and rescue medication use) over the 3rd pollen season. Results: 457 patients completed 3 consecutive seasons (2007, 2008 and 2009). Preliminary results of the 3rd pollen period showed a meanAAdSS±SD of 3.51±3.68 and 3.39±3.23 for the 4 and 2 months pre-seasonal treatment groups respectively, compared with 5.28±3.95 in placebo group. The efficacy of 300IR 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet seems to increase at every season, reaching a meanAAdSS improvement vs. placebo of 34% (median = 48%) in season 3 (4-month pre-seasonal treatment group). In this group, the mean AAdSS±SD was 3.79±3.95 in monosensitized patients and 3.31±3.49 in polysensitized patients. The treatment effect seemed to appear at lower pollen levels in the 2nd and 3rd seasons (booster effect). Treatment-related adverse events were mostly transient and mild to moderate in intensity, and their number decreased in the 2nd and 3rd treatment seasons. Conclusion: Three years of pre-coseasonal treatment with a 300IR 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet showed a sustained effect with statistical and clinically significant improvement of rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms in adults and a booster effect was observed. Efficacy was good in mono and in polysensitized patients. A potentially disease-modifying effect is now being investigated by discontinuing the SLIT and scoring the patients' symptoms for 2 additional seasons.
Efficacy and safety of timothy grass allergy immunotherapy tablet in North American adults; Background: Until now, the efficacy of grass sublingual immunotherapy has not been demonstrated in North America. This phase III trial investigated the efficacy and safety of daily administration of grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT) (oral lyophilisate, Phleum pratense, 2800 BAU) in allergic rhinocon-junctivitis (AR) subjects with or without asthma. Methods: 439 adults with Timothy grass pollen-induced AR were randomized 1:1 to once-daily grass AIT or placebo for approximately 16 weeks before and during the 2009 grass pollen season (GPS). Subjects used daily e-diaries to record AR symptoms from randomization through the end of the study. The use of symptomatic medications was also recorded in the e-diary during the GPS. The primary efficacy endpoint comprised the average total combined daily symptom and medication score (TCS) during the entire GPS. Key secondary endpoints were individual daily symptom score (DSS), daily medication score (DMS), and Rhinocon-junctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire (RQLQ[S]) with standardized activities. Immunologic endpoints included specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by monitoring adverse events (AEs). IRB approval was obtained for all sites, and each subject provided written informed consent. Results: 85% of the subjects were multisensitized. The grass AIT group showed a mean 20% improvement in TCS compared with the placebo group (P=0.005). Similar positive outcomes in the AIT group relative to placebo were demonstrated for the DSS (18%, P=0.015), DMS (26%, P=0.084), and RQLQ(S) (17%, P=0.022). In the grass AIT group, significant increases in IgG4 and IgE blocking factor were observed at peak and end of GPS compared with placebo (P<0.001). The majority of AEs were mild local application site reactions that were transient in nature, with no treatment-related serious AEs in active subjects, one treatment-related serious AE in a placebo subject, and no reports of anaphylactic shock. Conclusions: Grass AIT is effective in a predominantly multisensitized North American adult population with Timothy grass pollen-induced AR. Once-daily administration of grass AIT was well tolerated and will provide a new therapeutic modality for patients with Timothy and related grass pollen-induced allergy.
Carbamylated monomelic allergoids as a therapeutic option for sublingual immunotherapy of dust mite- and grass pollen-induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis: A systematic review of published trials with a meta-analysis of treatment using Lais® tablets; Lais® allergoid tablets contain allergens that are modified by carbamylation. Due to their modified chemical structure, they are suitable for sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) (13, 16, 17, 24). Based on their small molecule size of 12 to 40 kDa, they can be easily absorbed via the oral mucosa (1). In this review, we studied the efficacy of SLIT with carbamylated monomeric allergoid tablets in the treatment of grass pollen- and dust mite-induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis on the basis of symptom and medication score improvements. Following a selective internet and databank search, six trials - some placebo-controlled - regarding the treatment of grass pollen- (n = 266) and dust mite-induced (n = 241) allergic rhinoconjunctivitis were used to draw conclusions regarding the clinical efficacy of allergoid tablets. The primary endpoints in these trials were decreases in the need for allergy medications and/or reductions in the occurrence of rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms. Data was recorded from patient diaries regarding their symptoms and medications used and conclusions were then drawn about the effectiveness of and tolerance to Lais® tablets. The average improvement in symptom score in three trials of grass pollen allergy treatment was 34% in comparison to the placebo group. The treatment of dust mite-induced rhinoconjunctivitis produced an average symptom score improvement of 22% compared to the placebo or control groups. The intake of symptomatic PRN medication during allergoid tablet therapy declined. Treatment of grass pollen allergies and dust mite-induced rhinoconjunctivitis showed an average medication score improvement of 49% and 24%, respectively. Few side effects were documented in the trials and predominantly local effects were observed. Severe systemic side effects did not occur. On the basis of the trial results summarized in this review, we suggest that SLIT using Lais® sublingual tablets is an effective and well-tolerated form of treatment.
Five-grass pollen 300IR SLIT tablets: Efficacy and safety in children and adolescents; The efficacy and safety of five-grass pollen 300IR sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablets (Stallergènes SA, France) have previously been demonstrated in paediatric patients. This report presents additional data concerning efficacy at pollen peak, efficacy and safety according to age, nasal and ocular symptoms, use of rescue medication, satisfaction with treatment and compliance. Children (5-11 yr) and adolescents (12-17 yr) with grass pollen-allergic rhinoconjunctivitis were included in a multinational, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study and received either a 300IR five-grass pollen tablet or placebo daily in a pre- (4 months) and co-seasonal protocol. The severity of six symptoms (sneezing, rhinorrhoea, nasal congestion, nasal and ocular pruritis, and tearing) was scored, and rescue medication use was recorded daily during the pollen season. Patient satisfaction was recorded at the season end. A total of 161 children and 117 adolescents were evaluated (n = 267). 300IR SLIT was effective over the whole season (p = 0.0010) and at the pollen peak (p = 0.0009). The adjusted mean difference between 300IR and placebo groups was significant for both nasal (p = 0.0183) and ocular (p < 0.0001) symptoms. Rescue medication use was statistically lower in the SLIT group during the pollen season and at the pollen peak (both p < 0.05). More patients in the SLIT group were satisfied with their treatment compared to placebo (83.2% vs. 68.1%, p = 0.0030), and compliance was high (SLIT 93.9% of patients were compliant, placebo 94.8% of patients were compliant). SLIT was well tolerated by children and adolescents. 300IR five-grass pollen tablets are effective and safe during the pollen season and at the pollen peak in children and adolescents with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis. © 2010 John Wiley & Sons AS.
Molecular determinants of T cell epitope recognition to the common Timothy grass allergen; We investigated the molecular determinants of allergen-derived T cell epitopes in humans utilizing the Phleum pratense (Timothy grass) allergens (Phl p). PBMCs from allergic individuals were tested in ELISPOT assays with overlapping peptides spanning known Phl p allergens. A total of 43 distinct antigenic regions were recognized, illustrating the large breadth of grass-specific T cell epitopes. Th2 cytokines (as represented by IL-5) were predominant, whereas IFN-γ, IL-10, and IL-17 were detected less frequently. Responses from specific immunotherapy treatment individuals were weaker and less consistent, yet similar in epitope specificity and cytokine pattern to allergic donors, whereas nonallergic individuals were essentially nonreactive. Despite the large breadth of recognition, nine dominant antigenic regions were defined, each recognized by multiple donors, accounting for 51% of the total response. Multiple HLA molecules and loci restricted the dominant regions, and the immunodominant epitopes could be predicted using bioinformatic algorithms specific for 23 common HLA-DR, DP, and DQ molecules. Immunodominance was also apparent at the Phl p Ag level. It was found that 52, 19, and 14% of the total response was directed to Phl p 5, 1, and 3, respectively. Interestingly, little or no correlation between Phl p-specific IgE levels and T cell responses was found. Thus, certain intrinsic features of the allergen protein might influence immunogenicity at the level of T cell reactivity. Consistent with this notion, different Phl p Ags were associated with distinct patterns of IL-5, IFN-γ, IL-10, and IL-17 production. Copyright © 2010 by The American Association of Immunologists, Inc.
Influence of high-dose grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy on allergic eye and nose symptoms; Background: SLIT was shown to be dose-dependent and is accepted for grass pollenallergic patients (ARIA 2008 update). High-dose 6-grass pollen SLIT containing 40 μg grasses group five allergen in the maintenance dose was shown to be effective and safe in a randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled (DBPC) trial (Pfaar O/Klimek L. Ann Allergy Asthma Immunol 2008). Symptom and medication scores were reduced after only 3-4 months of SLIT and further decreased after 1.5 years of therapy. Methods: After a baseline grass pollen season, 185 grass-pollen allergic adults with rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma were treated with AllerSlit forte or placebo for 1.5 years in a randomized DBPC trial. All subjects recorded seven rhinoconjunctivitis and three asthma symptoms daily during (Table presented) grass pollen season. All patients had access to symptomatic treatment. Here we evaluated nasal and conjunctival symptom scores separately for the 42-day grass pollen evaluation period. Results: Data from 104 subjects (49 active, 55 placebo) were evaluated (main data set acc. to Pfaar/Klimek). Total medication score was reduced during the study for the active group while it remained unchanged for the placebo group (see Pfaar/Klimek). At baseline mean single symptom scores were comparable between the active and placebo groups. After 3-4 months of SLIT mean AUC of total nasal and total eye symptom scores as well as individual nasal (itching, sneezing, runny nose, obstruction) and conjunctival (itching, tear flow, redness) symptoms were markedly reduced in the active group compared to the placebo group. The mean differences increased further after 1.5 years of SLIT in table. Conclusion: AllerSlit forte has a high effect on all separate nose and eye symptoms compared to symptomatic treatment alone already after 3-4 months of SLIT which further increased in the following year. It is an effective treatment for rhinoconjunctivitis.

An assessment of sustained efficacy and safety of a 300IR five-grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy tablet in adults with grass pollen induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis during a 3-year treatment study; Background: In single-season Phase IIb/III clinical trials, once-daily pre- and co-seasonal treatment with a 300IR 5-grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet has been shown to be safe and effective in adults and children. The aim of this study was to evaluate the sustained efficacy and safety of pre- and co-seasonal treatment with 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet in adults with grass pollen induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Methods: Six hundred and thirty-three patients (aged between 18 and 51) were included in a DBPC, international, multicentre Phase III clinical trial s to evaluate the sustained efficacy and safety of the grass pollen SLIT tablet over three consecutive seasons. Patients were randomized to a placebo group and two active groups, receiving either 4 or 2 months of pre-seasonal treatment and co-seasonal treatment. Use of rescue medication was permitted. The primary efficacy endpoint was the Average Adjusted Symptom Score (AASS) during the third pollen season. Results: Four hundred and fifty-seven patients completed three consecutive seasons (2007, 2008, and 2009). Preliminary results of the 3rd pollen period showed a mean AASS ± SD in placebo group of 5.28 ± 3.946, in active groups of 3.51 ± 3.677 and 3.39 ± 3.232 for the 4 and 2 months preseasonal treatment groups respectively, giving a relative mean difference versus placebo of 34% and 36%, and a median of 48% and 50%. The differences in LS (Least Squares) mean [95% CI] (P value) were -1.78[-2.58,-0.98] (P < 0.0001) and -1.95[-2.75,-1.15] (P < 0.0001) respectively for the 4 and 2 months preseasonal treatment groups. The mean AASS in both active groups were significantly lower (P < 0.0001) in year 2 (median of 43% in both the 4 and 2 months preseasonal treatment groups) and in year 1(medians of 27% and 33% in the 4 and 2 months preseasonal treatment groups) versus placebo. SLIT tablets had an excellent safety profile. Treatment related adverse events were mild to moderate and transient
Ultra-short preseasonal immunotherapy of depiquick grass pollen extract compared to placebo in patients with type-I allergic rhinoconjunctivitis; Background: This clinical trial evaluates in a prospective, controlled, multicenter study an ultra-short preseasonal rush immunotherapy with a depigmented and glutaraldehyde polymerized allergenic extract of grass pollen (Depiquick Grass Mix) versus placebo in patients with rhinitis and/or rhinoconjunctivitis with/without allergic asthma caused by clinical relevant sensitisation against grass pollen. Efficacy and safety evaluation of the first and second treatment course will be presented. Methods: In the first treatment year 148 patients with a mean age of 33 years were treated 8 weeks before start of the grass pollen season with seven injections of 1000 DPP/ml of grass pollen extract (1 DPP is the result of depigmenting and polymerising 1 HEPL of allergenic extract.) administered within 5 weeks or with placebo. At day one 0.2 ml followed by 0.3 ml after 30 min were administered as initial up-dosing and followed by 0.5 ml at weeks 1-5 as maintenance treatment. In the second treatment year 146 patients were still within the study for another treatment course. The combined symptom and medication score for rhinitis and/or rhinoconjunctivitis was evaluated using ediaries based on software displaying a questionnaire with single questions on allergic symptoms and rescue medication. Results: After the first treatment course the median time weighted AUC of the combined symptom and medication score was 7.01 for placebo and 5.45 for actively treated patients a significant (P < 0.005) reduction of 22%. This was mainly due to a significant reduction of concomitant rhinitis/rhinoconjunctivitis medication consumption by 44% (P < 0.02). After the second treatment course median time weighted AUC of the combined symptom and medication score was 6.6 for placebo and 4.25 for actively treated patients a significant (P = 0.006) reduction of 36%. The medication consumption was reduced by 65% (P < 0.001) and the symptom score by 18% (P < 0.032). During first pollen season patients of the placebo group experienced 8.1% well day compared to 17.4% of well days in the Depiquick group and during the second season 9.2% and 21.4% respectively. Mild grade 1 systemic reactions occurred in 10.7% patients of the Depiquick group and in 6.7% patients of the placebo group. Conclusion: Ultra-short SIT with Depiquick grass pollen extract is clinically effective and safe already after six full doses. A second treatment course leads to further reduction of the combined symptom and medication score.
Efficacy and safety of grass monomeric allergoid sublingual immunotherapy: A randomised study comparing two different treatment regimens versus drug therapy alone; Background: Monomeric allergoid sublingual immunotherapy (allergoid SLIT) proved to be well tolerated, safe and effective in patients with respiratory allergy. A drawback is that the achievement of a clinical benefit using a standard regimen requires a long time before efficacy may be appreciated. Aim of this study was to investigate whether different regimens of allergoid SLIT (i.e. perennial versus preseasonal) may affect the clinical outcome. Methods: Thirty-three adult patients (mean age 29 ± 10.1 years, 13 F) with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis with/without mild intermittent asthma were enrolled into the study and randomised to receive either allergoid SLIT with a perennial regimen (group 1: 10 patients, 1000 AU/week for 2 years, for a total amount of 100 000 UA) or allergoid SLIT with a pre-seasonal regimen (group 2: 11 patients, 5000 AU/week for 10 weeks/year for 2 years, for a total amount of 104 000 UA) or drug therapy alone (group 3: 11 patients). At baseline and at the end of the second pollen season a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) was performed to assess the patients' well-being. Symptom score (sneezing, rhinorrea, nasal congestion, ocular symptoms, cough, asthma symptoms) and drug consumption (antihistamines, broncodilators, nasal, bronchial and oral corticosteroids) were also evaluated with monthly diary cards. Adverse reactions (ARs) were recorded. Results: One patient of group 1 dropped-out after the randomisation because of two mild ARs (paresthesia at the lips and sorethroat with burning). The statistical analysis was thus performed on 32 patients. There was a significant VAS improvement in both group 1 and 2 after one and 2 years of treatment compared to the baseline and group 3 values (P < 0.05). Furthermore, group 1 and 2 patients had less symptoms in comparison to group 3 at the second year, being the difference statistically significant (P < 0.05). In addition there was a significant reduction of drug consumption in group 1 and 2 versus group 3 at the second year (P < 0.05). No severe AR has been observed. Conclusion: Both perennial and pre-seasonal grass allergoid SLIT showed to be effective, safe and well tolerated.
Lack of association between clinical efficacy and cellular immune responses in the blood during grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy; Background: The efficacy of a 300-IR 5-grass-pollen SLIT tablet on rhinoconjonctivitis symptoms was confirmed in a double blind, placebo controlled study conducted in an allergen challenge chamber (ACC). We took advantage of this study to inquire whether changes in basophil activation and T cell responses can be used as biomarkers predicting efficacy. Methods: Changes in immunological markers were investigated in 82 patients with grass pollen-related allergic rhinoconjunctivitis who received active or placebo tablets sublingually once a day for four months. An allergen challenge with grass pollen was performed in the Vienna Challenge Chamber (VCC) at baseline, after 1 week, and after 1, 2 and 4 months of treatment. Blood samples were collected before treatment and after 2 and 4 treatment months for assessment of basophil activation (based on CD203c expression) and T cell responses (looking at proliferation, pheno-type, cytokine production and gene expression). Results: SLIT with a 5-grass-pollen tablet led to significant improvement (versus placebo) of 29.8% for the mean average rhinoconjunctivitis total symptom score (ARTSS) at end point. Clinical responders were defined as the 25% of patients with the highest relative improvement in symptom score from baseline, corresponding to at least a 48.9% decrease of ARTSS after treatment. No changes in basophil activation and T-cell proliferation were noticed for active and placebo groups in response to either the extract, Dac g 1 or Dac g 5 allergens, during treatment. Higher expression of Treg markers (eg Foxp 3) and lower expression of molecules associated with a Th1/Th2 profile were observed during treatment for both active and placebo groups. PMBCs from patients exposed to active treatment (versus placebo) displayed a lower production of TNF-alpha and a higher expression of IFN-gamma, with no differences between responders and non responders. All patients with a high symptom score before treatment responded to SLIT. However, neither basophil activation nor levels of T cell proliferation prior to treatment were predictive of efficacy. Conclusion: Basophil activation and T cell responses measured in the blood are not predictive of clinical responses induced by SLIT. Our results do not preclude that changes in cellular responses monitored in target respiratory mucosae can account for SLIT efficacy.
Heterogeneity of antibody responses among clinical responders following grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy; Background: Biomarkers of clinical efficacy are of major interest to monitor patients under specific sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT). To identify such surrogates, we investigated seric and mucosal antibody responses in the context of a randomised, double-blind placebo-controlled study conducted in an allergen challenge chamber. Methods: Changes in titres and affinity of allergen-specific IgE, IgG1-4, IgM and IgA were monitored in serum and nasal secretions in a cohort of 82 grass pollen allergic patients receiving once daily a sublingual 300IR grass pollen tablet or a placebo for 4 months. Average rhinoconjunctivitis total symptom score (ARTSS) was evaluated before and after 1 week, 1, 2 and 4 months of treatment, during allergen challenges in a pollen chamber. Results: A significant clinical response was observed in the group receiving the active treatment. The latter was documented in patients exhibiting distinct patterns of IgE sensitization (i.e. narrow, intermediate, broad) against Phl p 1 to 12 allergens. No neosensitization to grass pollen allergens was observed in the course of SLIT. Responders were defined as the 25% of patients with the highest relative improvement in symptom score from baseline, corresponding to at least a 48.9% decrease of ARTSS after treatment. Comparing antibody responses from patients belonging to each of the four following groups: Placebo responders/Placebo non responders/Active responders/Active non responders, we could establish that (i) Placebo responders exhibit clear grass pollen-specific antibody responses in nasal mucosa and (ii) Active responders are heterogeneous, encompassing both 'immunoreactive' patients with strong grass pollen-specific IgG, IgE and IgG4 responses (possibly blocking antibodies) induced during SLIT, as well as 'non immunoreactive' responders with no detectable immune responses distinguishing them from patients receiving placebo. Importantly, patients with high symptoms scores at inclusion were all improved under SLIT, although specific IgE levels prior to treatment were not predictive. Conclusion: SLIT with a grass pollen extract is similarly efficacious in patients exhibiting sensitization to single or multiple grass pollen allergens. Responders include both patients with strong seric allergen-specific antibody responses or lacking such responses. The latter observation suggests that additional immune mechanisms distinct from blocking antibodies are involved in SLIT efficacy.

Therefore, treating the underlying cause of allergy may represent an attractive modality for asthma prevention. The clinical development of specific immunotherapies: Specific methodological issues and clinical interpretation of results; Background: Historically, the safety and efficacy of specific immunotherapy (SIT) has been demonstrated in (mainly small-scale) studies and confirmed in meta-analyses. However, the inherent sources of heterogeneity (patient selection outside the pollen season, pollen count variations, long evaluation periods) observed in recent, large-scale studies of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablets mean that highly susceptible patients may not exhibit uniformly a sufficient level of symptoms during the study period to assess the efficacy of SIT. Methods: We performed randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational clinical trials of a 300IR pre- and co-seasonally administered 5-grass pollen SLIT tablet in adults and children with allergic rhinitis. The primary efficacy criterion was the mean average rhinoconjunctivitis total symptom score (ARTSS) over the pollen season, during which daily grass pollen counts were measured locally. To evaluate the potential relationship between disease severity and the treatment effect, investigating centres were divided in tiertiles according to their mean placebo group ARTSS. Results: Pre-seasonal inclusion of patients on the basis of retrospectively severe symptoms in the previous pollen season did not produce such a severely affected population in the study season. The apparent treatment effect of SLIT tablet was partially masked by the highly variable severity of symptoms during the whole pollen season (mean ARTSS in the adult placebo group: 4.93 ± 3.229). For the adult trial, the mean active versus placebo ARTSSs and the relative difference versus placebo (%) were 15%, 26% and 37% for the low, moderate- and high-disease severity tiertiles, respectively. In the paediatric trial, these values were 10%, 33% and 34%, respectively. Linear regression of individual daily RTSS scores and pollen counts in active and placebo groups also showed that the more severe the disease, the greater the treatment effect of SLIT tablet. Conclusion: Inherent sources of heterogeneity must be taken into account when interpreting the results of natural-exposure immunotherapy trials but none obscured the statistically significant treatment effect of SLIT. In a subgroup analysis, SLIT tablets' beneficial effect increased with disease severity.
The practice, safety and efficacy of immunotherapy in East Midlands; Background: Immunotherapy is known to be highly effective and to modify the natural history of allergic rhinitis and offer potential for long term disease remission. The aim of this study is to assess the practice, safety and efficacy of 10 years of specific immunotherapy at Glenfield Hospital, Leicester UK. Methods: A retrospective study was designed including the patients that received or were still receiving immunotherapy for allergic rhino-conjunctivitis associated or not with asthma. Results: Data from 70 patients (43 males, 27 females), age range 17-64, receiving specific grass pollen immunotherapy (63 patients), birch pollen or HDM immunotherapy were analyzed. Seasonal asthma was associated in 32 patients while six patients had perennial asthma and were offered SLIT. Most of the patients were offered SCIT (47 patients), SLIT had been available as an alternative type of desensitization since 2006 at Glenfield Hospital. Side effects to immunotherapy were reported by 45 patients, most of these side effects being local side-effects. SLIT was discontinued in three cases because of moderate to severe side effects. SCIT was associated in nine patients with immediate side effects, and in six patients with delayed side effects, most of them occurred during up-dosing protocol and being considered mild or moderate. Since January 2005, nine patients completed a 3 years course of immunotherapy and two patients discontinued immunotherapy for various reasons. In the seven patients, that completed SCIT in 2008, the disease severity according to ARIA classification was in four patients Intermittent mild, in one patient persistent mild and in two patients Persistent moderate-severe. Conclusions: Specific Immunotherapy in our patients with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis associated or not with asthma proved to have a reasonable safety profile. A clinical improvement during and after Immunotherapy was observed in most of the cases. More data are required in order to assess the long term efficacy of specific immunotherapy in our patients.
Does the basophil specific ectoenzyme E-NPP3 (CD203c) expression has a diagnostic value in pollen allergy?; Background: Skin tests, serum specific IgE results may sometimes be incompatible with the clinical history. Therefore, more sensitive and specific methods are needed in the diagnosis of allergy. The aim of this study was to investigate the value of the basophil activation test via CD203c expression in the diagnosis of pollen allergy. Methods: Thirty-one pollen allergic patients with compatible clinical history and positive skin prick tests and nine nonatopic healthy controls were examined. Grass mix specific IgE levels (Pharmacia CAP System) were measured in all subjects. The basophil activation using CD203c expression (Allergenicity Kit, Beckman Coulter, France) was determined outside the pollen season in peripheral blood after invitro stimulation with four different concentrations of grass pollen extract (Allergopharma Joachim Ganzer KG, Germany) by flow cytometer (Beckman Coulter, Cytomics FC500, USA). Results: The specificity for the CD203c molecule expression on activated basophils was found 100% at all allergen concentrations. The sensitivity was 100% with stimulation via 10 PNU (protein nitrogen unit) and one PNU. The sensitivities for 0.0001 PNU and 0.00001 PNU were 93% and 87%, respectively. Furthermore, a weak correlation found between specific IgE levels and activated basophils at 10 PNU and 1 PNU concentrations while a moderate correlation detected at 0.0001 PNU and 0.00001 PNU. Conclusion: According to the preliminary data, basophil activation seems to have a potential of gold standard among invitro tests in the diagnosis of pollen allergy. We suggest that it may be useful in assessment of the efficacy of allergen immunotherapy, and needs further studies.

Disease-modifying effect of the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet is sustained 2 years after treatment; Background: The sustained efficacy following completion of 3 years of treatment with the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Hørsholm, Denmark) was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase III trial. Adult subjects, with a clinical history of moderate to severe grass pollen allergy inadequately controlled by the use of symptomatic medications, were followed for 2 years after treatment. Methods: The analysis set included 241 subjects (137 active and 104 placebo, providing diary data during the season). Primary endpoints were the rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores during the entire grass pollen season 2 years after end of treatment. Further individual symptom scores and combined symptom and medication scores were analysed. Safety was assessed by means of adverse events, safety laboratory assessments and vital signs. Results: Two years after treatment completion, the active group showed a 31% reduction in median rhinoconjunctivitis symptom score (mean reduction 25%, P = 0.004) when compared with placebo over the entire grass pollen season. The six individual symptom scores were all in favour of active treatment (see Table 1). The reduction in rhinoconjunctivitis medication score was in favour of active treatment, yet not significant (median reduction 21%, mean reduction 20%, P = 0.114). The combined symptom and medication score showed a 33% reduction in medians (mean reduction 27%, P = 0.003) in favour of active treatment. The level of efficacy on all endpoints was similar to the previous two seasons (all P -values for test of differences between years were >0.05). No safety issues were identified during the follow-up. Conclusion: Two years after completing a 3-year treatment period with the SQ-standardised grass AIT, a sustained, significant, disease-modifying effect was observed. This trial is the first large-scale, randomised, (Table presented) placebo-controlled trial showing long-term disease modification after grass AIT treatment.
Sublingual grass pollen immunotherapy is associated with increases in sublingual Foxp3-expressing cells and elevated allergen-specific immunoglobulin G4, immunoglobulin A and serum inhibitory activity for immunoglobulin E-facilitated allergen binding to B cells; Background: The mechanisms of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) are less well understood than those of subcutaneous immunotherapy (SCIT). Objectives: To determine the effects of grass-pollen SLIT on oral mucosal immune cells, local regulatory cytokines, serum allergen-specific antibody subclasses and B cell IgE-facilitated allergen binding (IgE-FAB). Methods: Biopsies from the sublingual mucosa of up to 14 SLIT-treated atopics, nine placebo-treated atopics and eight normal controls were examined for myeloid dendritic cells (mDCs) (CD1c), plasmacytoid dendritic cells (CD303), mast cells (AA1), T cells (CD3) and Foxp3 using immunofluorescence microscopy. IL-10 and TGF-β mRNA expression were identified by in situ hybridization. Allergen-specific IgG and IgA subclasses and serum inhibitory activity for binding of allergen-IgE complexes to B cells (IgE-FAB) were measured before, during and on the completion of SLIT. Results: Foxp3+ cells were increased in the oral epithelium of SLIT- vs. placebo-treated atopics (P=0.04). Greater numbers of subepithelial mDCs were present in placebo-treated, but not in SLIT-treated, atopics compared with normal controls (P=0.05). There were fewer subepithelial mast cells and greater epithelial T cells in SLIT- compared with placebo-treated atopics (P=0.1 for both). IgG1 and IgG4 were increased following SLIT (P<0.001). Peak seasonal IgA1 and IgA2 were increased during SLIT (P<0.05). There was a time-dependent increase in serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB in SLIT-treated atopics. Conclusions: SLIT with grass pollen extract is associated with increased Foxp3+ cells in the sublingual epithelium and systemic humoral changes as observed previously for SCIT. © 2010 Blackwell Publishing Ltd.

Pre-seasonal specific immunotherapy in rhino-conjunctivitis versus placebo; RATIONALE: Evaluate the efficacy and safety of specific immunother- apy with grass pollens (SIT) in a blind controlled study with a pre-seasonal treatment during 5 years in patients with seasonal rhino-conjunctivitis. METHODS: We selected 36 patients with rhino-conjunctivitis, who were sensitised to grass pollens, 18 male and 18 female with moderate or severe grade of rhino-conjunctivitis according ARIA. All patients received information about forecasts and total pollens count in their residential area. The placebo group had received physiological serum daily in sublingual drops during 7 weeks before spring. The ANOVA test and T test was used for analyse differences between groups and for analyse the correlations the Spearman method was used. RESULTS: A significant improvement was observed regarding symptoms in all patients as in throughout the treatment period in those who specific immunotherapy was performed. The severity of symptoms was smaller in SIT group that in the placebo group. The tolerance and safety of immunotherapy was excellent and just in 0.8%. CONCLUSIONS: The short-term and pre-seasonal immunotherapy, subcutaneous for patients sensitised to grass pollens is effective and safety. Long-term clinical efficacy in grass pollen-induced rhinoconjunctivitis after treatment with SQ-standardized grass allergy immunotherapy tablet; Background: Sustained and disease-modifying effects of sublingual immunotherapy have never before been confirmed in a large-scale randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial. Clinical parameters to evaluate the efficacy of allergen-specific immunotherapy in allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Calculation of "well days" as an adjunct concept; Efficacy monitoring of immunotherapy (IT) is performed to adjust the therapy according to the patients reactions, to collect data for scientific studies and to evaluate the efficacy of IT. A decrease of allergy symptoms and of drug use are the main efficacy parameters obtained by using the symptom-medication-score (SMS). For this, allergy diaries, skin tests and nasal provocation testing are most suitable. During the period of documentation pollen exposition should be monitored with Burkhard traps. In cytologic measures specific cell activation markers and specific lymphokine-profiles can be evaluated. For the reason of their expense, those methods are restricted to scientific investigations and only rarely for routine diagnostics. Numerous clinical trials have investigated the efficacy and safety of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) using symptom- and medication-scores. The results suggest that high allergen doses are necessary to achieve adequate efficacy. An alternative approach to assess the outcome of allergen-specific immunotherapy was investigated by a new high-dose sublingual 6-grass pollen preparation. This sublingual immunotherapy significantly improved the symptom-medication-score (SMS) in a double-blind, placebo-controlled trial by counting "well days" (days without intake of any rescue medication and a reduction of the symptom-score) during the grass pollen season. This post-hoc analysis showed that SLIT for 1.5 years resulted in a significantly higher number of "well days" than with placebo. These results reflect the high improvement in symptom-medication-score. "Well days" can therefore be regarded as a further variable to measure efficacy of allergen-specific immunotherapy. © 2010 Dustri-Verlag Dr. Karl Feistle.

Treatment with the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet in children above and below the age of 10 - Efficacy, safety and immunological response; Purpose: Treatment with the SQ-standardised grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), GRAZAX® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark), is efficacious and well-tolerated in grass allergic patients. Younger children are often perceived as good candidates for immunotherapy, and post-hoc analyses to explore possible differences between younger and older children were performed for a grass AIT paediatric phase III trial. Methods: Of 253 children aged 5-16 years with grass allergy were randomised 1:1 to receive grass AIT or placebo prior to and during the grass pollen season 2007. Symptomatic medications were provided as rescue medication to both groups. The population was divided into two age groups comprising 140 children aged 5-10 years and 113 children aged 11-16 years. The effect of age on efficacy was also analysed by including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis. Results: Analysis of the difference between active and placebo for each age group showed a slightly higher relative difference in symptom score for the younger children compared with the older, 26% versus 19%. The difference between active and placebo was statistically significant for the 5-10 year-olds (P = 0.047), although the trial was powered for analysis of the population as a whole. Including age as a covariate in the symptom score analysis indicated age to have no influence on the efficacy, as demonstrated by a p-value of 0.6 for the interaction between age and treatment. In the AIT group the percentage of adverse events assessed as treatment-related was nominally lower for the younger group than for the older, 32% versus 40%. The percentages of adverse events assessed as moderate or severe in the active group were similar for the two age groups. For both active and placebo, the overall number of adverse events was higher in the younger group than in the group of older children. The immunological response (IgE, IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor) was similar for the two age groups. There was a tendency for the younger group towards an increased response in IgE-blocking factor at the onset of the grass pollen season, both in the active and the placebo group. Conclusions: The phase III trial, investigating the SQ-standardised grass AIT, demonstrated once-daily administration to be clinically efficacious and well-tolerated in children between 5 and 16 years. Comparison of two age groups revealed similar efficacy and tolerability after treatment with grass AIT independent of age.
SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet in children induces clinical and immunological responses similar to those seen in adults; Background: Clinical results and serum antibody responses were investigated after treatment with the grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), GRAZAX® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark) in children and results compared to those obtained after the same treatment in adults. Purpose: In randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled multi-centre trials in children (5-16 years) and adults (18-65 years), AIT were initiated prior to the grass pollen season, and once-daily treatment was continued until the end of the season. All participants had a clinical history of moderate to severe grass allergy. The daily rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medications scores during the grass pollen season and the levels of Phleum pratense (Table presented) specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor (i.e. serum components competing with IgE for allergen binding) in serum samples before, during and after the season were assessed. Results: Statistically significant improvements were found for the rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores in the grass AIT group relative to placebo. The improvements were comparable to those seen for adults with similar grass pollen exposure (see Table 1). In children, specific IgG4 was significantly increased from the initial level at all assessments after treatment initiation in the active group (P < 0.001). The increase after 7.3 months was 32 U/L. In adults, the corresponding increase was 45.7 U/L (P < 0.001). The IgE-blocking factor increased in the grass AIT groups in both children and adults (P < 0.001 in both trials). In children the difference from the initial level in the active group was 0.25 after 7.3 months, while in adults the difference was 0.15 after 8 months. Conclusions: SQ-standardised grass AIT treatment is effective in children with grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis. Improvements in efficacy scores and immunological responses were similar to those in a comparable trial in adults, in whom a disease modifying effect was shown in terms of clinically relevant benefits that were sustained for at least one year after 3 years of treatment.
Sustained, disease-modifying effect of grass allergy tablet immunotherapy 1 year after a 3-year treatment period; Background: The sustained efficacy with the SQ-standardised grass allergy immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax® (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T, ALK-Abello, Denmark), 1 year after a 3-year treatment period, was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled, phase III trial. Participants were adults with a history of moderate to severe grass pollen induced rhinoconjunctivitis inadequately controlled by symptomatic medications. Methods: The full analysis set included 283 participants (157 AIT, 126 placebo). All participants had free access to symptomatic medications during the grass pollen season. Primary and key secondary efficacy endpoints were rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores, quality of life, and percentages of symptom and medication free days. Immunological endpoints included specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by adverse events. Results: 1 year after treatment, the grass AIT group showed a 26% reduction in mean rhinoconjunctivitis symptom scores (P < 0.001) and a 29% reduction in medication scores (P = 0.022) when compared with placebo. The corresponding median reductions were 31% and 52%. This level of efficacy was similar to the efficacy during treatment. The mean difference in percentages of symptom and medication free days during the entire season was 27% (P= 0.038) (median difference 49%) in favour of AIT. The grass AIT group also reported better rhinoconjunctivitis quality of life (P= 0.004). No safety issues were identified. Conclusion: Significant and clinically relevant efficacy of grass AIT was sustained 1 year after a 3-year treatment period. This is the first time evidence of disease modification by sublingual tablet immunotherapy has been demonstrated in a large-scale, randomised, placebo-controlled trial.
The SQ-standardized grass allergy immunotherapy tabletis well tolerated over 3 years of treatment; Introduction: Traditionally, allergen-specific immunotherapy has been administered via subcutaneous injections or sublingual drops. Recently, a new formulation administered sublingually, allergy immunotherapy tablets (AIT), has emerged. Many large double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trials have demonstrated AIT to be well tolerated and efficacious in reducing rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms and the use of rescue medications. We investigated the nature, severity and duration of the most common adverse events (AEs) associated with treatment with an SQ-standardized grass AIT (Phleum pratense, 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK, Denmark) during a trial conducted over a 3 year period. Methods: Subjects with grass-allergy (n=634) were enrolled in a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multinational trial, of which 351 continued in a 2-year extension. Approval was obtained from the local IRBs and written informed consent obtained from all subjects. Subjects were randomized 1:1 to grass AIT or placebo. All AEs were collected and rated as mild, moderate or severe. AEs were assessed as treatment-emergent if the start date was on or after the date of the first tablet intake. Reactions occurring for a limited period of time each day on consecutive days were reported as one event. Results: The most frequent AEs after grass AIT were oral pruritus (164 subjects, 191 events), mouth edema (65 subjects, 70 events), ear pruritus (41 subjects, 42 events) and throat irritation (34 subjects, 37 events). The majority of these AEs were mild (84%) or moderate (13%) and transient of nature. There was a sharp decrease in the percentage of subjects reporting these AEs within the first weeks ofthe trial (figure). Approximately 10% continued to have oral itching during the entire trial, typically lasting 10 minutes after tablet administration. Withdrawal due to these local AEs was low (n=6, 1%). Conclusions: The most frequent AEs after grass AIT were local reactions affecting the mucosal membranes in mouth, ear and throat. The majority of AEs were mild and generally subsided shortly after treatment initiation, indicating development of tolerance. A small proportion of the subjects experienced local AEs daily upon tablet administration. However, the rate of withdrawals due to local AEs was low, indicating that grass AIT was well tolerated and these side effects were acceptable to most patients. (Figure Presented).

Continuous use of Grazax was modelled, with the effects of treatment captured in terms of symptom management, resource utilisation and allergic asthma development. Data inputs were drawn from published studies, databases and published clinical trials. RESULTS: The use of Grazax plus symptomatic medications compared to standard care showed an improvement in patient outcomes
Efficacy of grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy for three consecutive seasons and after cessation of treatment: The ECRIT study; Background: Data supporting a carry-over effect with sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) are scarce. This randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study evaluated the efficacy, carry-over effect and safety of grass pollen SLIT using co-seasonal treatment. Methods: Patients (7.9-64.7 years) with grass pollen allergy received ultra-rush titration with increasing doses (30, 90, 150 and 300 IR) of a 5-grass pollen mixture every 20 min at the start of the pollen seasons, followed by 300 IR daily until the end of the pollen seasons. A baseline season (no SLIT) was followed by three consecutive treatment seasons and one follow-up season. Symptoms, medication and adverse events were documented and specific immunoglobulin (Ig)E and IgG4 measured. Results: Data were analysed for 183 of the 213 randomized patients. Mean treatment duration varied between seasons (81.8-92.7 days). Combined scores (symptoms and medication) improved progressively across treatment seasons (up to 44.7% improvement for SLIT compared with baseline) and fluctuated between -11.3% and -14.8% for placebo (P < 0.05). Similar changes were observed for symptom scores, with a successive decrease of 39.7% (SLIT) and fluctuations between +13.6% and -1.51% for placebo (P < 0.05). Combined score (P = 0.0508) and symptom score improvements (P = 0.0144) with SLIT continued during follow up. Increases in specific IgG4 observed in the first season were sustained for SLIT vs placebo throughout treatment (P = 0.0001). Titration and daily SLIT were well tolerated. No serious systemic or anaphylactic reactions were reported. Conclusions: Seasonal SLIT with ultra-rush titration is well tolerated and effective from the first treatment season onwards. These data indicate a carry-over effect of seasonal SLIT. © 2008 The Authors.

Recombinant allergens for following antibody-responses during immunotherapywith a grass pollen vaccine and after five years of discontinuation; Objectives: Follow-up of antibody responses in serum samples from grass pollen allergic patients having received one course of injection immunotherapy (SIT) with an aluminium hydroxide-adsorbed grass pollen extract (n=12) or only anti-inflammatory treatment (n=7). Serum samples were taken before treatment, after five months of treatment and after five years. Methods: Specific IgE, IgG1-IgG4, IgM, IgA and light chain responses were monitored using purified recombinant timothy grass (i.e., Phl p 1, Phl p 2, Phl p 5, Phl p 6, Phl p 7, Phl p 12, Phl p 13)- and birch pollen (Bet v 1) allergens. Results: After five months of SIT but not after inflammatory treatment we found increases of IgG1 > IgG4 > IgG2 antibody responses in particular for the major grass pollen allergens Phl p 1, Phl p 5, Phl p 6 and Phl p 2 but no relevant increases against the other tested allergens (Phl p 7, Phl p 12, Phl p 13, Bet v 1). No relevant induction of allergen-specific IgA, IgM or IgG3 responses were found in the SIT group. The increases of allergen-specific IgG responses were accompanied by allergen-specific light chain binding involving both kappa and lambda chains in the SIT group. An inhibition of allergen-dependent basophil degranulation was only obtained with sera from the SIT-group containing therapy-induced allergen-specific IgG antibodies but not with sera from the group having received only antiinflammatory treatment. A decrease of Phl p 1- and Phl p 5-specific IgE levels was found in the SIT group after the grass pollen season but not in the group of patients receiving only anti-inflammatory treatment. After five years allergen-specific (Phl p 1, Phl p 5) IgE and IgG antibody levels in the SIT group had returned to baseline levels. Conclusion: Our results demonstrate that only SIT but not anti-inflammatory treatment can prevent boosts of allergen-specific IgE production in allergic patients. Thus only SIT but not anti-inflammatory treatment has disease-modifying effects.
Response to sublingual immunotherapy with grass pollen extract: Monotherapy versus combination in a multiallergen extract; Background: To date, there have been no randomized, double-blind studies showing the effectiveness of sublingual immunotherapy with multiple allergens. Objective: The purpose of this study was to examine whether the efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) with standardized timothy extract was reduced by combination with other allergen extracts. Methods: A single-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial with SLIT was conducted. After an observational grass season, SLIT was administered for 10 months to 54 patients randomized to 1 of 3 treatment arms: placebo, timothy extract (19 μg Phl p 5 daily) as monotherapy, or the same dose of timothy extract plus 9 additional pollen extracts. Symptom and medication scores were collected and titrated nasal challenges, titrated skin prick tests, specific IgE, IgG4 and cytokines release by timothy-stimulated lymphocyte proliferation were performed. Results: Perhaps because of a very low grass pollen season in 2008, there were no significant differences in medication or symptom scores in either treatment group compared with placebo. Compared with placebo, in the timothy monotherapy group, thresholds for titrated nasal challenge and skin prick tests (P = .03 and P = .001, respectively), and serum-specific IgG4 levels (P = .005) significantly increased, and IFN- γ levels decreased (P = .02), whereas in the multiallergen group, there was significant improvement only in the titrated skin prick tests (P = .04) which was less than in the monotherapy group. There were no significant differences between the 2 active groups in any outcome measure, and both active groups experienced more adverse events than placebo. There were no systemic reactions. Conclusion: Improvement in multiple relevant outcomes strongly suggests that SLIT with timothy extract alone was effective
Allergen-specific immunotherapy in allergic rhinitis and asthma. Mechanisms and proof of efficacy; Appeared at the beginning of the 20th century, allergen-specific immunotherapy (SIT) has long been used in allergic rhinitis and asthma without any knowledge of its mechanisms of action or any tangible proof of its efficacy. However, from the beginning of the era of evidence-based medicine, a number of placebo-controlled studies have been published and reached a sufficient number to assess the cellular events induced by SIT and allow meta-analysis to provide guidelines based on proofs. Controlled studies and meta-analysis concerned not only subcutaneous immunotherapy but also the sublingual route, demonstrating an effect of SIT on symptoms and medication use. Most recently sublingual tablets were proposed in allergic rhinitis. This paper reviews the mechanisms of SIT, the evidence of efficacy of SIT from the injective to the sublingual route and reminds the current guidelines. © 2009 Elsevier Ltd. All rights reserved.
Safety and tolerability of an ultra short course subcutaneous immunotherapy formulation in a large randomised phase III trial; Background: The efficacy and tolerability of ultra short course subcutaneous immunotherapy (uSCIT) adjuvanted with monophosphoryl lipid A (MPL) for grass pollen allergy was assessed in a large, randomised, placebo-controlled trial. Here, we closely examine the adverse event (AE) and tolerability profile of uSCIT in this study. Methods: This trial randomised 1028 grass pollen-allergic subjects 1 : 1 to receive active treatment with four preseasonal injections of uSCIT or placebo. Local and systemic reactions and changes in laboratory parameters were monitored during the 24 h following each injection, and AEs were recorded during the therapy period and the rest of the trial. Results: The safety population included 1027 patients (one discontinuation in the placebo group before treatment). During treatment, 361 subjects (70%) in the uS-CIT group and 241 subjects (47%) in the placebo group experienced one or more AEs (P < 0.001). There were no major differences between groups with respect to clinical parameters. Over the four-injection course in the uSCIT group, 63-70% of patients reported no local or systemic AEs, 27-32% reported only local AEs, and 3-7% reported systemic AEs. No major differences were seen between the two treatment groups with regard to other types of AE (systemic events i.e. headache, nasopharyngitis and upper respiratory tract infection). The most common AEs with uSCIT were local symptoms (pain, pruritus, swelling and erythema), and the vast majority of AEs in both groups (98%) were mild or moderate in severity. During the therapy period, AEs with at least a possible relationship to study medication were reported in 299 subjects (58%) who received uSCIT and 135 subjects (26%) who received placebo (P < 0.001). Treatment-related AEs occurred almost exclusively during the therapy period
Foxp3 is increased in the sublingual mucosa after grass pollen sublingual immunotherapy and is accompanied by increases in serum IgG4 and serum inhibitory activity for allergen-IgE binding to B cells; Background: Sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) using an SQ-standardised extract of Phleum Pratense (ALK Abello, Horsholm Denmark, daily drops containing 25 μg Phleum p5 daily for 12-18 months) resulted in improvements in patients' overall assessment of hayfever severity, suppression of the late skin response and an increase in serum IgG4 level, but no change in T cell numbers, CD1a+ Langerhans' cells or IL-12 mRNA expression in the sublingual mucosa (Lima M, Clin Exp Allergy 2002). We now report measurements of dendritic cell subsets, mast cells, T cells and Foxp3 as a marker of T regulatory cells in the sublingual mucosa and serum inhibitory activity for allergen-IgE binding to B cells (IgE-FAB). Methods: Cryostat/paraformaldhyde sections from the sublingual mucosa from 14 SLIT-treated, nine placebo treated and eight normal controls were immunostained for myeloid dendritic cells (CD1c), plasmacytoid dendritic cells (CD303), mast cells (AA1) and T cells (CD3) using dual-immunofluorescence microscopy. Sections from approximately six subjects from each group were also immunostained for Foxp3 and IL-9. Positive cells were counted blind and expressed per mm2 in the epithelium and subepithelium. Serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB was measured at different time points. Results: Epithelial myeloid dendritic cell (mDC) numbers were similar in each group. There were greater subepithelial mDCs in placebo treated patients than normal controls (P = 0.05). Few plasmacytoid dendritic (pDC) cells were seen in any group and were largely confined to the subepithelium. There were possibly fewer subepithelial mast cells and more epithelial T cells in SLIT-compared to placebo-treated subjects (P = 0.1 for both). Foxp3+ cells within the epithelium were present in five of seven SLIT-treated patients compared to zero of six placebo-treated patients (P = 0.014, Chi-Square test) and Foxp3 co-localised to T cells in two subjects in whom dual immunofluorescence was informative. There was a time-dependent increase in serum inhibitory activity for IgE-FAB (P < 0.001), maximal at 6 weeks when compared to placebo-treated patients. Conclusion: These data suggest that SLIT may operate through similar mechanisms to SCIT and is associated with recruitment of T cells including possible Foxp3+ Tregs to the sublingual epithelium, a possible reduction in subepithelial mast cells and is accompanied by increases in serum IgG4 and IgG-associated inhibition of IgE-facilitated allergen presentation.
Sustained, disease-modifying effect of grass allergen tablet immunotherapy 1 year after completion of treatment; Background: The sustained efficacy following withdrawal of ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75,000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abello, Hørsholm, Denmark) was investigated in a randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase III trial. Participants were followed for 1 year after completion of a 3-year period of daily treatment. Participants were adults with a clinical history of moderate to severe grass pollen allergy inadequately controlled by the use of symptomatic medication. Methods: The full analysis set included 283 participants (157 active, 126 placebo) who entered the follow-up investigation 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment. Efficacy endpoints comprised rhinoconjunctivitis symptom and medication scores, quality of life, and percentages of symptom and medication free days. Immunological endpoints included serum levels of grass pollen specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor. Safety was assessed by means of adverse events. Results: One year after completion of treatment the active group showed a 26% reduction in rhinoconjunctivitis symptom scores (P < 0.001) and a 29% reduction in medication scores (P < 0.02) when compared with placebo over the entire grass pollen season. This level of sustained efficacy between active and placebo groups was not significantly different compared to the previous season, i.e. during treatment. The difference in percentages of symptom and medication free days during the entire season was 27% (P = 0.04) in favour of AIT. Participants in the active group also reported better rhinoconjunctivitis quality of life both during the entire grass pollen season and during the peak season. Sustained clinical benefit after treatment completion was accompanied by persistent immunological changes as observed for subcutaneous immunotherapy. Six mild adverse events were assessed as treatmentrelated. Eight serious events (four in each group) were all assessed as unlikely related to treatment. Conclusion: This double-blind analysis 1 year after completion of a 3-year period of daily all year round treatment showed significant effects on the primary and key secondary endpoints, thereby demonstrating sustained efficacy with ALK grass AIT following completion of treatment. This is the first time that evidence of disease modification by allergen tablet immunotherapy has been shown in a large-scale, randomised, placebo-controlled trial. No safety issues were identified.
Efficacy and safety of a 300IR five-grass pollen SLIT tablet in children and adolescents with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis; Background: The efficacy and safety of a five-grass pollen 300IR sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) tablet (Stallergenes SA, France) has recently been confirmed in children and adolescents with grass pollen allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Secondary and complementary analyses on the trial's intention-to-treat (ITT) population were performed for the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS), the six Average Individual Symptom Scores (AISSs) and for safety parameters according to age class (5 to 11 and 12 to 17). Methods: Two hundred and seventy-eight children (5-17 years of age) with grass pollen-related rhinoconjunctivitis were included in a multinational, randomized, doubleblind, placebo-controlled phase III trial. They were treated with once-daily five grasspollen SLIT tablets or placebo tablets 4 months prior to and then throughout the pollen season. Each symptom was evaluated daily by the patients (with parental assistance, for the youngest) on a four-point scale from 0 (absent) to 3 (severe). Results: The difference between 300IR and placebo tablets was significant for rhinorrhea (P = 0.038), nasal congestion (P = 0.0012), ocular pruritus (P < 0.0001) and watery eyes (P = 0.0008). Improvements in sneezing and nasal pruritus were not statistically significant. Patients aged 12 to 17 had higher ARTSSs and AISSs than those aged 5 to 11 years whatever the treatment group and the analysed period (pollen season or pollen peak). In both age classes (12-17 and 5-11), the ARTSS was lower in the 300IR group (3.77 and 2.94, respectively) than in the placebo group (4.98 and 4.08, respectively). A complementary subgroup analysis on safety parameters revealed a similar safety profile of the 300IR five-grass pollen sublingual tablet in age subgroups 5-11 and 12-17, as well as in sub-goups 5-7 and 8-11. Conclusion: Five-grass pollen tablet immunotherapy has a significant effect on eye and nasal symptoms such as nasal congestion and rhinorrhea and was in this study an effective treatment for grass polleninduced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis in children and adolescents, independently of age class. Also, the same dose can be administered to allergic children and adolescents aged 5-17 years without any influence on the tolerability of the treatment.

Methods: Patients with grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis were randomized into either active (300 IR SLIT tablets, od) (Oralair® Grasses Stallergenes, France) or placebo groups treated for 4 months out of the season, without rescue medication usage. The improvement in disease specific quality of life is sustained 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment with the a grass allergen immunotherapy tablet; Background: Grass pollen induced allergic rhinoconjunctivitis affects 15-25% of the European population. Seventy percent of all allergic patients report that their condition significantly impairs their quality of life (QoL). Three years of all year round treatment with the ALK grass allergen immunotherapy tablet (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2,800 BAU, ALK-Abello), has previously been shown to reduce rhinoconjunctivitis symptoms and medication use. Importantly, this was accompanied by a significant improvement in the QoL of the patients. In this trial we investigated whether the improvement in QoL was sustained 1 year after completion of 3 years treatment. Methods: The trial was a parallel-group, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial in which patients were randomised to receive 3 years of either active treatment (n = 157) or placebo (n = 126), followed by 1 year of follow-up to investigate the long-term effects after treatment withdrawal. Disease specific QoL was assessed after the 1st year of follow-up based on Juniper's Rhinoconjunctivitis Quality of Life Questionnaire (RQLQ), which was completed by the patients weekly during the entire grass pollen season. The RQLQ defines QoL based on seven individual domains (activities, sleep, non-nose/eye symptoms, practical problems, nasal problems, eye problems, and emotional) assessed on a scale from 0-6 (0: 'not troubled', 6: 'extremely troubled'). Results: The overall RQLQ score (mean of the seven domains) for the entire season (average length = 65 days) was significantly improved in patients receiving the ALK grass AIT compared with placebo (23%, P = 0.004, see table). This improvement was even more pronounced during the peak grass pollen season (length = 15 days, difference = 28%, P = 0.001). All seven domains were improved in patients receiving active treatment (data not shown). Conclusion: These results demonstrate that the previously observed improvement of disease specific QoL in adult patients suffering from grass allergy receiving 3 years of daily all year round treatment with the ALK grass AIT is sustained 1 year following completion of treatment indicating a disease modifying effect of therapy.

Quality of life benefits for patients with seasonal grass pollen-allergic rhinoconjunctivitis receiving ultra short course subcutaneous immunotherapy (uSCIT) with MPL adjuvant; Background: Seasonal allergic rhinoconjunctivitis is known to affect quality of life and the ability to perform everyday tasks. This effect has been demonstrated using allergy-specific quality of life (QoL) questionnaires, which have consistently recorded lower QoL in patients with the condition than in controls. This multicentre, double-blind, placebo-controlled study examined the effects of ultra short course subcutaneous immunotherapy (uSCIT) with monophosphoryl lipid A (MPL) adjuvant on QoL for patients with seasonal grass pollen-allergic rhinoconjunctivitis. Methods: A total of 1028 patients were randomised to receive four preseasonal injections of uSCIT with MPL adjuvant (n = 514) or placebo (n = 514). A validated rhinoconjunctivitis questionnaire (RQLQ) was used to evaluate QoL. Seven domains were assessed: activities, sleep, non-nose/eye symptoms, ocular symptoms, practical problems, nasal symptoms and emotional wellbeing. The scores were recorded when the first dose of uSCIT was administered (an assessment of the previous pollen season), 2-3 weeks after the final dose, in the middle of the grass pollen season and then again after the season (assessing the previous week). Results: As expected, in both groups, RQLQ scores were lowest at the mid-season assessment. No significant difference was demonstrated between treatment groups, pre or postseason. During the pollen season, the overall RQLQ scores for patients treated with uSCIT were significantly better than those recorded by the subjects who had received placebo (P = 0.0153). In addition to this, better scores were recorded by the uSCIT-treated group than by the placebo group during the pollen season for the individual domains of activities (P = 0.0045), nonnose/eye symptoms (P = 0.020), practical problems (P = 0.023), nasal symptoms (P = 0.053) and emotional wellbeing (n = 0.0097)
Grass allergen tablet immunotherapy in children induces IgG4 antibody and 4IgE-blocking responses similar to those seen in adults; Background: Serum antibody responses were investigated after ALK grass allergen tablet immunotherapy (AIT), Grazax (Phleum pratense 75 000 SQ-T/2800 BAU, ALK-Abelló) in children and results compared to those obtained after the same treatment in adults. Methods: In randomised, double-blind, placebo-controlled multi-centre trials in children (5-16 year) and adults (18-65 year), AIT was initiated prior to the grass pollen season, and once-daily treatment was continued until the end of the season. All participants had a clinical history of moderate to severe grass allergy. The levels of Phleum pratense specific IgG4 and IgE-blocking factor (i.e. serum components competing with IgE for allergen binding) were assessed in serum samples before, during and after the grass pollen season. Results: In children, specific IgG4 was significantly increased from the initial level at all assessments after treatment initiation in the active group (P < 0.001). The increase after 7.3 months of treatment was 32 U/l. In adults, 8 months of treatment led to an increase in the active group of 45.7 U/l from the initial level (P < 0.001). The IgE-blocking factor increased in the active AIT groups in both children and adults (significantly different treatment effect in the active groups compared to the placebo groups, P < 0.001 in both trials). In children the difference from the initial level in IgE-blocking factor in the active group was 0.25 after 7.3 months, while in adults the difference was 0.15 after 8 months. The adult placebo group showed no significant change while the paediatric placebo group showed a slight increase in IgE-blocking factor during the grass pollen season. The clinical treatment effect in children by means of the rhinoconjunctivitis symptom score and the percentage of well days was also similar to that observed in adults when looking at comparable pollen exposure periods. Conclusion: These data confirm comparable elevations in serum IgG4 antibodies and IgE-blocking activity in children compared with adults following successful treatment with ALK grass AIT. In addition, the higher seasonal elevations in IgE-blocking activity in placebo-treated children compared with adults, suggest that children may establish a more tolerogenic rather than allergic immune response during the grass pollen season. This observation suggests that the use of AIT in childhood may ameliorate the ongoing natural course of the disease more efficiently than AIT during adulthood.

We aimed to study the allergen profile of grass pollen allergic patients from our area in Madrid (Spain), since geographical variation may exist and regional data are scant. Methods: We selected prospectively 138 grass pollen allergic patients with rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma during the grass pollen season and positive skin prick test to a grass pollen mixture. None of the patients had previously received allergen immunotherapy. Sera were tested (ImmunoCAP, Phadia) to Phleum pratense and to its recombinant allergens rPhl p 1-2-5b-6-7-11-12, and to nPhl p 4. Results: All the patients presented a positive (≥0.35 kU/l) CAP to Phleum pratense whole extract, median 26.30 kU/l (interquartile range [IQR] 9.81-62.35). The effect of the matched pairs method on the measurement of treatment effect in a trial of ultra short course specific immunotherapy; Background: A common problem during the assessment of specific immunotherapy (SIT) in patients with allergic rhinoconjunctivitis is incomplete data from patient diaries during the pollen season. Such data gaps may be filled using the matched pairs method, but this process has the potential to distort the data and invite misleading conclusions. Here we compare whole-population, matched-pair data from a large Phase III trial with the subgroup of patients for whom complete data were available to assess the effect of the matched pairs method. Methods: In a large, randomised, Phase III trial, 1028 patients with moderate-to-severe allergic rhinoconjunctivitis to grass pollen received ultra short course specific immunotherapy (uSCIT) or placebo. Efficacy was measured using a combined symptom and medication score (CSMS) from patient diary data collected during the four peak weeks of pollen exposure. We compared data for the whole population, which utilised a matched pairs method to fill data gaps, with data from the subset of subjects for whom complete data were available. Results: There were 343 subjects for whom 28 diary entries were available during the four peak weeks of the pollen season (177 uSCIT, 166 placebo). In both the whole population and the complete data subgroup, uSCIT was found to be significantly more effective than placebo, with improvements in the CSMS of 13.4% (P = 0.0038) and 26.9% (P = 0.0031) for the whole population and complete data groups, respectively. There was a similar pattern for symptom scores alone (eyes and nose), with improvements for uSCIT compared with placebo of 10.9% (P = 0.0132) and 24.7% (P = 0.0181) for the whole population and complete data groups, respectively. Conclusion: The disparity in the primary endpoint between the whole population and the subgroup with complete data was considerable. Although a significant benefit for uSCIT compared with placebo was detected in each set of data, the size of the effect was approximately doubled when complete data were used to compare the treatment groups. It is therefore apparent that using the matched pairs method to compensate for incomplete data may not improve the precision of the estimate of treatment effect for the whole population, in this case yielding a 50% underestimate of treatment efficacy in approximately one third of subjects. This phenomenon represents a significant challenge in the assessment of SIT formulations for allergic rhinoconjunctivitis.
Efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy with a high-dose 6-grass pollen preparation progressively increases in the third treatment year; Background: Efficacy of sublingual immunotherapy (SLIT) depends on a sufficiently high maintenance dose as recently confirmed by the ARIA workshop report (2008 update). Often SLIT trials last for at most 2 years, and only few data are available about the effect of further treatment years. Progressive efficacy of a high-dose Single Dose grass pollen preparation has been shown in a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicentre trial for the first two treatment years. The preparation was standardized to the content of 40 μg grasses group 5 allergens in the maintenance dose. Aim of this study was to investigate the effect of a third treatment year. Methods: After a baseline period in 2003 grass pollen-allergic patients (rhinoconjunctivitis with or without asthma) received treatment with AllerSlit forte or placebo for two consecutive years (2004 and 2005). Data from 39 of the former 49 actively treated patients in the Main Data Set (MDS) were available in the third, open treatment year (2006). Symptom and medication scores, and the number of 'well days' (days with a symptom score ≤4 and without use of any rescue medication) were evaluated. Results: Symptom and medication scores were further improved during the third treatment year in comparison to the year before. The percentage of patients who did not need any rescue medication during the 42-day observation period in the grass pollen season continuously increased during the study: 0% at baseline, 19% at the end of the double-blind phase in 2005 to 31% in the third treatment year. Additionally, the median number of 'well days' increased during the 3 years: from 6/42 days at baseline pollen season, and 19.5/42 days after two treatment years in 2005 to 35/42 'well days' after 3 years of SLIT. The high-dose preparation was well tolerated with mainly mild adverse reactions. No serious drugrelated adverse events occurred. Conclusion: The third year of SLIT with the high-dose grass pollen preparation caused an additional benefit for the patients. Allergic symptoms and the intake of rescue medication were further reduced during the grass pollen season. The high number of 'well days' affirms the progressive efficacy.

Methods: Three efficacy endpoints (the Average Rhinoconjunctivitis Total Symptom Score (ARTSS), the Average Rescue Medication Score (ARMS) and the Average Combined Score (ACS)) were calculated for two clinical trials (VO34.04 and VO52.06, in which rescue medication was allowed) and a pollen chamber trial (VO56.07A, in which rescue medication was prohibited) of the ORALAIR sublingual tablet for treating grass pollen rhinoconjunctivitis.
